\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 68\quad\small\texttt{(el6-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 68 (el6-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A graph of \( \ln N \) against \( t \) has equation \( \ln N = 0.2t + 5 \). Find: (a) the initial value; (b) the doubling time.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) t = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln N = 5 \implies N = e^5 = 148.4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( t = 0 \). Apply \( e^{\square} \) (not \( 10^{\square} \)) because the graph uses \( \ln \), not \( \log_{10} \). Keeping the base consistent is the key trap to avoid.

\textit{Step 2: (b) When N doubles}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(2N_0) = 0.2t + 5 \implies \ln 2 = 0.2(t - 0) \implies t = \frac{\ln 2}{0.2} = 3.47
\end{gathered}\]
Doubling means \( \ln(2N_{0}) - \ln N_{0} = \ln 2 \); on the linearised graph this is a rise of \( \ln 2 \), so the time taken is \( \ln 2 / \text{gradient} \). For any exponential, doubling time is constant.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 148.4 (b) 3.47 time units
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 69\quad\small\texttt{(el6-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 69 (el6-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Two models are proposed for data: Model A \( y = ax^n \) and Model B \( y = kb^x \). Plotting \( \log y \) vs \( \log x \) gives a curve but \( \log y \) vs \( x \) gives a line. Which model fits?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Analysis}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Model B fits: } y = kb^x
\end{gathered}\]
A power model gives a line on \( \log y \) vs \( \log x \); an exponential model gives a line on \( \log y \) vs \( x \). Here the second linearisation is the straight one, so the data is exponential.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Model B: y = $kb^x$
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 70\quad\small\texttt{(el6-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 70 (el6-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A graph of \( \log y \) against \( \log x \) passes through \( (0, 1.5) \) and \( (2, 4.5) \). Find the equation \( y = ax^n \), then find: (a) \( y \) when \( x = 100 \); (b) \( x \) when \( y = 1000 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find n}
\[\begin{gathered}
n = \frac{4.5 - 1.5}{2 - 0} = 1.5
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient \( = \Delta(\log y)/\Delta(\log x) = n \).

\textit{Step 2: Find a}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log a = 1.5 \implies a = 10^{1.5} \approx 31.6
\end{gathered}\]
The first point \( (0, 1.5) \) lies on the \( \log y \)-axis (where \( \log x = 0 \)), so it gives \( \log a \) directly.

\textit{Step 3: (a) x = 100}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 31.6 \times 100^{1.5} = 31.6 \times 1000 = 31600
\end{gathered}\]
\( 100^{1.5} = (10^{2})^{1.5} = 10^{3} = 1000 \). A clean integer power makes this easy without a calculator.

\textit{Step 4: (b) y = 1000}
\[\begin{gathered}
1000 = 31.6x^{1.5} \implies x^{1.5} = 31.6 \implies x = 31.6^{2/3} = 10.0
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate \( x^{1.5} \), then raise to the power \( 2/3 \) (the reciprocal of \( 1.5 \)) to solve for \( x \). Since \( 31.6 \approx 10^{1.5} \), we get \( x \approx (10^{1.5})^{2/3} = 10 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad y = $31.6x^{1.5}$; (a) 31600 (b) x = 10
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]
\begin{scope}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 71\quad\small\texttt{(el6-071)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 71 (el6-071)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = e^{x} \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the key features.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-intercept: } e^{0} = 1 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0 \text{ as } x \to -\infty
\end{gathered}\]
Any exponential \( y = a^{x} \) with \( a > 1 \) passes through \( (0,\,1) \) since \( a^{0} = 1 \), and tends to \( 0 \) (but never reaches it) as \( x \to -\infty \), giving a horizontal asymptote at \( y = 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Sketch the curve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{x} \text{ is increasing for all } x; \quad y > 0 \text{ everywhere}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve is always positive and increases without bound --- steeply for \( x > 0 \), shallowly for \( x < 0 \). At \( x = 1 \) it reaches \( e \approx 2.72 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, 1)\) and \(\displaystyle (1, e)\), with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 72\quad\small\texttt{(el6-072)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 72 (el6-072)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = \ln x \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the key features.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-intercept: } \ln 1 = 0 \quad \text{asymptote: } x = 0 \text{ as } x \to 0^{+}
\end{gathered}\]
The natural log is the inverse of \( e^{x} \): it is only defined for \( x > 0 \), passes through \( (1,\,0) \) since \( \ln 1 = 0 \), and goes to \( -\infty \) as \( x \to 0^{+} \), giving a vertical asymptote at \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Sketch the curve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \ln x \text{ is increasing for all } x > 0; \quad \ln e = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Growth is slow once past \( x = 1 \) --- for example \( \ln e \approx 1 \), \( \ln 10 \approx 2.30 \). The curve is the reflection of \( y = e^{x} \) in the line \( y = x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (1, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (e, 1)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \); defined only for \(\displaystyle  x > 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 73\quad\small\texttt{(el6-073)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 73 (el6-073)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{On the same axes, sketch \( y = e^{x} \) and \( y = e^{-x} \). State the relationship between the two curves.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the key features of each.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{x}:\ (0,\,1),\ \text{increasing} \qquad y = e^{-x}:\ (0,\,1),\ \text{decreasing}
\end{gathered}\]
Both pass through \( (0,\,1) \) because \( e^{0} = 1 \). \( e^{x} \) grows as \( x \) increases; \( e^{-x} \) decays as \( x \) increases (because the exponent gets more negative).

\textit{Step 2: Relate them by symmetry.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{-x} = e^{(-1)\cdot x}
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( -x \) reflects the curve in the \( y \)-axis, so \( y = e^{-x} \) is the mirror image of \( y = e^{x} \) about the \( y \)-axis. Both have horizontal asymptote \( y = 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Both curves pass through \(\displaystyle (0, 1)\) with asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \); \(\displaystyle  y = e^{-x} \) is the reflection of \(\displaystyle  y = e^{x} \) in the \(\displaystyle  y \)-axis.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The curve sketched with its intercepts and asymptote labelled.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 74\quad\small\texttt{(el6-074)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 74 (el6-074)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = e^{x} - 1 \), labelling the \( x \)- and \( y \)-intercepts and the asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{x} - 1 \text{ is a vertical translation of } y = e^{x} \text{ down by } 1
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting a constant from \( y \) shifts the whole curve down by that amount; the shape and steepness are unchanged, only the vertical position changes.

\textit{Step 2: Find the new intercepts and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } e^{0} - 1 = 0 \quad x\text{-int: } e^{x} = 1 \Rightarrow x = 0 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = -1
\end{gathered}\]
The curve now passes through the origin since both intercepts coincide at \( (0,\,0) \). The horizontal asymptote shifts from \( y = 0 \) down to \( y = -1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Curve through the origin \(\displaystyle (0, 0)\) with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = -1 \); the standard exponential shifted down by \(\displaystyle  1 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 75\quad\small\texttt{(el6-075)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 75 (el6-075)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = \ln(x + 2) \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept, \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \ln(x + 2) \text{ is a horizontal translation of } y = \ln x \text{ to the left by } 2
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( x + 2 \) shifts the curve in the \( -x \) direction by \( 2 \). The vertical asymptote moves with it from \( x = 0 \) to \( x = -2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find the intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-int: } \ln(x+2) = 0 \Rightarrow x + 2 = 1 \Rightarrow x = -1 \qquad y\text{-int: } \ln 2 \approx 0.69
\end{gathered}\]
For the \( x \)-intercept, set \( y = 0 \) and use \( \ln 1 = 0 \). For the \( y \)-intercept, evaluate at \( x = 0 \), giving \( \ln 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Curve through \(\displaystyle (-1, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (0, \ln 2)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = -2 \); the standard \(\displaystyle  \ln \) shifted left by \(\displaystyle  2 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 76\quad\small\texttt{(el6-076)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 76 (el6-076)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = 2^{x} \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the key features.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } 2^{0} = 1 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0 \text{ as } x \to -\infty
\end{gathered}\]
Any exponential \( y = a^{x} \) with \( a > 1 \) has the same general shape: passes through \( (0,\,1) \) and tends to \( 0 \) as \( x \to -\infty \). The base \( 2 < e \), so \( 2^{x} \) grows more slowly than \( e^{x} \).

\textit{Step 2: Sketch the curve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^{1} = 2, \quad 2^{2} = 4, \quad 2^{-1} = \tfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Plot a few values and join with a smooth increasing curve. Always positive, never crosses the \( x \)-axis.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, 1)\) and \(\displaystyle (1, 2)\), with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 77\quad\small\texttt{(el6-077)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 77 (el6-077)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = \log_{10} x \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the key features.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-int: } \log_{10} 1 = 0 \quad \text{asymptote: } x = 0 \quad \log_{10} 10 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Any log \( y = \log_{a} x \) with \( a > 1 \) passes through \( (1,\,0) \) and has vertical asymptote \( x = 0 \). Growth is slower than \( \ln x \) since \( \log_{10} = \ln/\ln 10 \) and \( \ln 10 \approx 2.30 \).

\textit{Step 2: Sketch the curve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10} 10 = 1, \quad \log_{10} 100 = 2, \quad \log_{10} 0.1 = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Defined only for \( x > 0 \). Note how slowly the curve climbs once past \( x = 1 \) --- it takes a tenfold increase in \( x \) to add \( 1 \) to \( y \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (1, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (10, 1)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The curve sketched with its intercepts and asymptote labelled.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 78\quad\small\texttt{(el6-078)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 78 (el6-078)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = -e^{x} \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -e^{x} \text{ is a reflection of } y = e^{x} \text{ in the } x\text{-axis}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiplying \( y \) by \( -1 \) flips the curve about the \( x \)-axis: every positive output becomes a negative output of the same magnitude.

\textit{Step 2: Find intercepts and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } -e^{0} = -1 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0 \text{ as } x \to -\infty
\end{gathered}\]
Now \( y \) is always negative, decreasing without bound as \( x \) increases. The asymptote \( y = 0 \) is approached from below.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Decreasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, -1)\), always negative, with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The curve sketched with its intercepts and asymptote labelled.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 79\quad\small\texttt{(el6-079)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 79 (el6-079)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = e^{2x} \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote. Compare its steepness with \( y = e^{x} \) at \( x = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{2x} \text{ is a horizontal compression of } y = e^{x} \text{ by factor } \tfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( 2x \) compresses the curve horizontally by \( \tfrac{1}{2} \), so growth happens twice as fast: \( e^{2x} \) at \( x = 1 \) equals \( e^{x} \) at \( x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find intercepts and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } e^{0} = 1 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0 \quad e^{2(1)} = e^{2} \approx 7.39
\end{gathered}\]
The \( y \)-intercept and asymptote are unchanged from \( y = e^{x} \); only the rate of growth changes. At \( x = 1 \), this curve has already reached \( e^{2} \), where \( e^{x} \) only reaches \( e \approx 2.72 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, 1)\) and \(\displaystyle (1, e^{2})\), with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \); grows twice as fast as \(\displaystyle  y = e^{x} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 80\quad\small\texttt{(el6-080)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 80 (el6-080)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = e^{x} + 2 \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{x} + 2 \text{ is } y = e^{x} \text{ translated up by } 2
\end{gathered}\]
Adding a constant to \( y \) shifts the whole curve vertically; the shape and steepness are unchanged.

\textit{Step 2: Find intercepts and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } e^{0} + 2 = 3 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 2 \text{ as } x \to -\infty
\end{gathered}\]
There is no \( x \)-intercept since \( e^{x} > 0 \) for all \( x \), so \( y \geq 2 \) always. The horizontal asymptote shifts up from \( y = 0 \) to \( y = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, 3)\) with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 2 \); no \(\displaystyle  x \)-intercept.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 81\quad\small\texttt{(el6-081)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 81 (el6-081)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = e^{-x} + 1 \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{-x} + 1 \text{ is } y = e^{-x} \text{ translated up by } 1
\end{gathered}\]
Start from the standard decay curve \( y = e^{-x} \) (reflection of \( e^{x} \) in the \( y \)-axis), then shift everything up by \( 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find intercepts and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } e^{0} + 1 = 2 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 1 \text{ as } x \to \infty
\end{gathered}\]
The curve decreases from large positive values on the left towards \( y = 1 \) on the right. There is no \( x \)-intercept since \( e^{-x} > 0 \) gives \( y > 1 \) always.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Decreasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, 2)\) with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 1 \); no \(\displaystyle  x \)-intercept.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 82\quad\small\texttt{(el6-082)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 82 (el6-082)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = \ln(2x) \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(2x) = \ln 2 + \ln x
\end{gathered}\]
Using the product law of logs, \( \ln(2x) \) is just \( \ln x \) shifted vertically up by the constant \( \ln 2 \approx 0.693 \). The shape and asymptote of \( \ln x \) are unchanged.

\textit{Step 2: Find intercept and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-int: } \ln(2x) = 0 \Rightarrow 2x = 1 \Rightarrow x = \tfrac{1}{2} \quad \text{asymptote: } x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The vertical asymptote stays at \( x = 0 \) because the transformation acts on \( y \), not on \( x \). The \( x \)-intercept moves to \( x = \tfrac{1}{2} \) since \( 2x = 1 \) there.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (\tfrac{1}{2}, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (1, \ln 2)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \); equivalent to \(\displaystyle  \ln x \) shifted up by \(\displaystyle  \ln 2 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 83\quad\small\texttt{(el6-083)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 83 (el6-083)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = \ln x + 1 \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \ln x + 1 \text{ is } y = \ln x \text{ translated up by } 1
\end{gathered}\]
Vertical translation by \( +1 \); shape and vertical asymptote at \( x = 0 \) are unchanged.

\textit{Step 2: Find intercept and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-int: } \ln x + 1 = 0 \Rightarrow \ln x = -1 \Rightarrow x = e^{-1} = \tfrac{1}{e} \quad \text{asymptote: } x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Solve \( y = 0 \) by taking \( e^{\square} \) of both sides; this gives \( x = 1/e \approx 0.368 \). The \( x \)-intercept shifts left from \( 1 \) since the curve has moved up.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (\tfrac{1}{e}, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (1, 1)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 84\quad\small\texttt{(el6-084)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 84 (el6-084)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = \ln(-x) \), stating its domain and labelling any asymptote and intercept.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Determine the domain.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x > 0 \implies x < 0
\end{gathered}\]
A logarithm requires a positive argument, so \( -x > 0 \) forces \( x < 0 \). The curve only exists to the left of the \( y \)-axis.

\textit{Step 2: Recognise the transformation and find features.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \ln(-x) \text{ is a reflection of } y = \ln x \text{ in the } y\text{-axis}
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( -x \) reflects the standard \( \ln x \) curve in the \( y \)-axis. The asymptote stays at \( x = 0 \); the \( x \)-intercept moves from \( (1,\,0) \) to \( (-1,\,0) \) since \( \ln(-(-1)) = \ln 1 = 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Defined only for \(\displaystyle  x < 0 \); curve through \(\displaystyle (-1, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (-e, 1)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \); reflection of \(\displaystyle  \ln x \) in the \(\displaystyle  y \)-axis.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 85\quad\small\texttt{(el6-085)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 85 (el6-085)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = 3 - e^{x} \), labelling the \( x \)- and \( y \)-intercepts and the asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformations.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3 - e^{x} = -e^{x} + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Two transformations of \( y = e^{x} \): reflect in the \( x \)-axis (the \( -e^{x} \) part), then translate up by \( 3 \). The asymptote at \( y = 0 \) reflects to itself, then shifts up to \( y = 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find intercepts and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } 3 - e^{0} = 2 \quad x\text{-int: } e^{x} = 3 \Rightarrow x = \ln 3 \approx 1.10 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
For the \( x \)-intercept, solve \( y = 0 \) and take \( \ln \) of both sides. The curve approaches \( y = 3 \) from below as \( x \to -\infty \) and decreases without bound as \( x \to \infty \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Decreasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, 2)\) and \(\displaystyle (\ln 3, 0)\), with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 3 \); a reflection-and-shift of \(\displaystyle  y = e^{x} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 86\quad\small\texttt{(el6-086)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 86 (el6-086)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = e^{x - 1} \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{x-1} \text{ is } y = e^{x} \text{ translated right by } 1
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( x - 1 \) shifts the curve in the \( +x \) direction by \( 1 \). The asymptote at \( y = 0 \) is unchanged because it is horizontal.

\textit{Step 2: Find the intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } e^{0-1} = e^{-1} = \tfrac{1}{e} \approx 0.368 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
No \( x \)-intercept since \( e^{x-1} > 0 \) always. The curve still passes through the point one unit right of the original \( (0,\,1) \): namely \( (1,\,1) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, \tfrac{1}{e})\) and \(\displaystyle (1, 1)\), with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 87\quad\small\texttt{(el6-087)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 87 (el6-087)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = \ln(x - 1) \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \ln(x - 1) \text{ is } y = \ln x \text{ translated right by } 1
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( x - 1 \) shifts the whole curve in the \( +x \) direction by \( 1 \). The vertical asymptote shifts with it from \( x = 0 \) to \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find intercept and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-int: } \ln(x-1) = 0 \Rightarrow x - 1 = 1 \Rightarrow x = 2 \quad \text{asymptote: } x = 1 \quad \text{domain: } x > 1
\end{gathered}\]
Defined only for \( x > 1 \) because the log requires a positive argument. The intercept moves from \( (1,\,0) \) to \( (2,\,0) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (2, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (1+e, 1)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \); defined only for \(\displaystyle  x > 1 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The curve sketched with its intercepts and asymptote labelled.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 88\quad\small\texttt{(el6-088)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 88 (el6-088)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = 2\ln x \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept and any asymptote. Compare with \( y = \ln x \) at \( x = e \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the transformation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2\ln x \text{ is a vertical stretch of } y = \ln x \text{ by factor } 2
\end{gathered}\]
Multiplying \( y \) by \( 2 \) stretches every output to twice its value; the shape is the same but the curve climbs faster. The vertical asymptote at \( x = 0 \) is unaffected by a vertical stretch.

\textit{Step 2: Find intercept and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-int: } 2\ln x = 0 \Rightarrow \ln x = 0 \Rightarrow x = 1 \quad \text{asymptote: } x = 0 \quad 2\ln e = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The \( x \)-intercept is unchanged at \( (1,\,0) \) --- multiplying \( 0 \) by anything is still \( 0 \). At \( x = e \) this curve reaches \( 2 \), where \( \ln x \) only reaches \( 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve through \(\displaystyle (1, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (e, 2)\), with vertical asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \); twice as tall as \(\displaystyle  y = \ln x \) for every \(\displaystyle  x > 0 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 89\quad\small\texttt{(el6-089)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 89 (el6-089)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch the curve \( y = 2^{-x} \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and any asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite as a decay.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^{-x} = \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{x}
\end{gathered}\]
A negative exponent inverts the base, so \( 2^{-x} \) is the same as \( (1/2)^{x} \) --- a decaying exponential with base less than \( 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find intercept and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } 2^{0} = 1 \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0 \text{ as } x \to \infty
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through \( (0,\,1) \) and decays towards the \( x \)-axis as \( x \) increases. At \( x = 1 \) it reaches \( \tfrac{1}{2} \); at \( x = -1 \) it climbs to \( 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Decreasing curve through \(\displaystyle (0, 1)\) and \(\displaystyle (1, \tfrac{1}{2})\), with horizontal asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Using Logarithmic Graphs 90\quad\small\texttt{(el6-090)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using Logarithmic Graphs 90 (el6-090)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{On the same axes, sketch \( y = \ln x \) and \( y = \log_{10} x \). Which curve climbs faster and why?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify shared and distinct features.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Both pass through } (1,\,0); \quad \text{both have asymptote } x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Every \( y = \log_{a} x \) with \( a > 1 \) has the same \( x \)-intercept \( (1,\,0) \) and the same vertical asymptote \( x = 0 \); only the rate of growth differs.

\textit{Step 2: Compare growth rates using change-of-base.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10} x = \frac{\ln x}{\ln 10} \approx \frac{\ln x}{2.30}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log_{10} x \) is just \( \ln x \) scaled vertically by \( 1/\ln 10 \approx 0.434 \), so it climbs slower. At \( x = e \), \( \ln x = 1 \) but \( \log_{10} x \approx 0.434 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Both pass through \(\displaystyle (1, 0)\) with asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \); \(\displaystyle  y = \ln x \) climbs faster because \(\displaystyle  \log_{10} x = \frac{\ln x}{\ln 10} \) and \(\displaystyle  \ln 10 \approx 2.30 > 1 \).
\end{answerbox}

\node[red!70!black,scale=0.68,anchor=center,inner sep=1pt] at (6.93,3.867) {$\log_{10}x$};
\fill[red!75!black] (1.085,2.9) circle (2pt);
\node[below right,scale=0.68] at (1.085,2.9) {$(1,0)$};
\end{tikzpicture}
}
\end{center}
\small The curve sketched with its intercepts and asymptote labelled.\normalsize
